People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me. I really appreciate it. I, um... I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill, because it's really not that bad, but... well, it's been brought to my attention that I have a bit of trouble just staying in my chair. 

Like during our staff meetings at school, or even just at dinner with my family when everyone else is seated. I just... I keep getting up. I'll suddenly feel like I need to refill everyone's coffee, or I'll pop up to check a handout, or I'll just walk to the back of the room. I always tell myself I'm just being helpful or stretching my legs, but... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, nothing I get up for is ever really urgent. 

It's a little embarrassing when people point it out, and I certainly don't want to be disruptive. I just feel this sudden urge to be on my feet. I hope that's an okay reason to come in? I just wanted to make sure I'm doing the right thing by getting it checked out.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I really hope it's alright to say that, but I actually pride myself on being quite careful with my work. 

I mean, when you're grading fifty essays in a weekend, you really have to make sure you're reading every word properly, you know? I wouldn't want to give a student the wrong grade because I was rushing or being careless. I always double-check my math on the grade book, too. I just... Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, I always try to be a very good listener. It's so important to make people feel heard, don't you think?

I just wanted to make sure I'm doing the right thing by getting it checked out.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble paying attention to details or staying focused when you’re doing work or reading?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I hope it's okay to say that, I just... I haven't really noticed any issues with my focus. \n\nAs a teacher, I spend quite a lot of time grading essays and reading over lesson plans, and I feel like I'm actually quite good at catching all the little details. I can sit and read for quite a while, it's just... well, it's just my body that wants to be up and moving, if that makes sense? I really don't have trouble concentrating on the actual work itself. \n\nDoes that... does that answer your question properly? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Has this tendency to get up and move around been a pattern since childhood and in most settings, not just recently at work or home?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has. I mean, I don't want to make it sound like I was a bad child or anything, because I really did try to be good! But... yes, it's been this way for as long as I can remember. \n\nEven when I was a little girl, my report cards would always say that I was bright, but... well, that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother—bless her heart, she was so patient with me—she used to joke that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. \n\nI always just thought I had a lot of energy, you know? I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terror in the classroom. I really did want to please my teachers. Is it... is it helpful to know that it's been happening since I was little? No, I really don't think I do. I hope that's the right answer, I just... I'm actually quite organized, I think. \n\nI mean, as a teacher, you sort of have to be, right? I always have my keys on a little lanyard, and my phone stays right in my purse, and I keep all my grading rubrics in very neat, color-coded folders. I really try my best to keep track of everything because I'd hate to inconvenience anyone or hold up a meeting because I couldn't find a paper. \n\nSo, no, I don't really lose things. I hope that's okay? I try so hard to make sure everything is done correctly because I wouldn't want anyone to think I wasn't doing my job well. \n\nSo, no, I don't really make careless mistakes. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not being difficult by saying no to some of these things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused when someone is speaking directly to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, I always try to be a very good listener. I would just feel awful if someone thought I was ignoring them or that I didn't care about what they were saying. I really value being respectful like that. \n\nSo, I don't think I have trouble focusing when people talk to me. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to be honest with you, and I really do try my best to listen well.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, well... I wouldn't call them *problems*, exactly. I mean, I really do try to be a good colleague and a good friend to everyone.

Oh, well... I really do try to give everything my full attention, especially when it's something important like my students' work or a faculty meeting. I wouldn't want anyone to think I'm slacking off or not taking my responsibilities seriously!

I wouldn't say it's *hard* for me, exactly... just that I have to remind myself to refocus every now and then. Is that normal? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for.

Oh, goodness, no. I really don't think so. I mean, as a teacher, paying attention to detail is practically my entire job! I would be absolutely mortified if I handed back a graded paper with a careless mistake on it.

I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no. No, I really don't think I do. I hope that's the right answer, I just... I'm actually quite organized, I think. \n\nI mean, as a teacher, you sort of have to be, right? I always have my keys on a little lanyard, and my phone stays right in my purse, and I keep all my grading rubrics in very neat, color-coded folders. I really try my best to keep track of everything because I'd hate to inconvenience anyone or hold up a meeting because I couldn't find a paper. \n\nSo, no, I don't really lose things. I hope that's okay? I know sometimes these things go hand in hand, but I really do try to stay on top of my belongings.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes when you’re doing work or other tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I really hope it's alright to say that, but I actually pride myself on being quite careful with my work. \n\nI mean, when you're grading fifty essays in a weekend, you really have to make sure you're reading every word properly, you know? I wouldn't want to give a student the wrong grade because I was rushing or being careless. I always double-check my math on the grade book, too. I just... I try so hard to make sure everything is done correctly because I wouldn't want anyone to think I wasn't doing my job well. \n\nSo, no, I don't really make careless mistakes. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not being difficult by saying no to some of these things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused when someone is speaking directly to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, I always try to be a very good listener. It's so important to make people feel heard, don't you think? \n\nWhen a student or a parent is talking to me, or even my colleagues in the staff room, I really make a point to look them in the eye and nod so they know I'm paying attention. I would just feel awful if someone thought I was ignoring them or that I didn't care about what they were saying. I really value being respectful like that. \n\nSo, I don't think I have trouble focusing when people talk to me. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to be honest with you, and I really do try my best to listen well.\n\nDoctor: Do these symptoms cause problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't call them *problems*, exactly. I mean, I really do try to be a good colleague and a good friend to everyone. \n\nBut... um... I suppose... well, maybe it has started to affect things just a little bit. At work, and in my community. It's just that sometimes in staff meetings, or at my book club... I can tell that people sort of lose patience with me. I hate admitting that to you, it's actually quite hard to say out loud because I don't want to sound like a nuisance. I go to these things wanting to participate and be supportive, but lately, I end up leaving feeling embarrassed instead of connected to everyone. \n\nI try so hard not to be a bother, you know? I hope it's alright that I'm sharing that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you have to sit still?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... um, I suppose I do move just a little bit, but I wouldn't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything! \n\nIt's just... well, sometimes when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays, or, oh dear, even when I'm in church, I do catch myself tapping my foot or clicking my pen. I tend to shift around in my seat quite a bit, too. I always have to sort of gently remind myself to just... settle down and be still. \n\nI just have a bit of nervous energy, I suppose. I hope that doesn't sound disrespectful, especially about being in church—I really do try my absolute best to sit quietly so I don't distract anyone else. Does that count as what you're asking about? I hope I'm answering these properly for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often blurt out answers before a question has been finished?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... at least, I certainly hope not! I really do try my best to wait until someone is completely finished before I speak. As a teacher, it's just so important to model good manners for the students, don't you agree? \n\nI would feel absolutely terrible if I interrupted someone or made them feel rushed. I always want to be polite and let people have their say. So, no, I don't think I blurt things out like that. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just really try to be mindful of other people's feelings.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks for long periods, even when the task is important or something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really do try to give everything my full attention, especially when it's something important like my students' work or a faculty meeting. I wouldn't want anyone to think I'm slacking off or not taking my responsibilities seriously!\n\nBut, um... I suppose... sometimes my mind does wander just the tiniest bit. If I'm sitting down with a very large stack of papers, or if a meeting goes on for quite a long time, I might find myself... oh, making a little to-do list in my head or thinking about what I need to pick up at the grocery store. \n\nBut I always pull myself right back! I really do make a conscious effort to stay on track because I'd hate to miss something crucial. I wouldn't say it's *hard* for me, exactly... just that I have to remind myself to refocus every now and then. Is that normal? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you have to pay close attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really don't think so. I mean, as a teacher, paying attention to detail is practically my entire job! I would be absolutely mortified if I handed back a graded paper with a careless mistake on it. \n\nI really do take my time and double-check my work. I suppose everyone makes a tiny error here and there—nobody's perfect, right?—but I certainly wouldn't say I do it often. I try very, very hard to be thorough and precise. I hope that doesn't make me sound boastful! I just take a lot of pride in doing my job well. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh dear... well, I know I just said I try very hard not to do that, and I really do! But... I suppose if I'm being completely honest, there are times when I might get just a little too enthusiastic. 

Like in group discussions—especially at my book club or during our curriculum meetings at school. Sometimes, if someone is sharing a thought, the response is just suddenly right there in my head, and... well, I might accidentally jump in before they've completely finished. It just sort of blurts out before I can stop myself! 

I always feel dreadful about it and apologize immediately, of course. I just get so swept up in the collaboration and the sharing of ideas. I don't mean to be impolite at all. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude? I really do try to be a good listener most of the time!

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, I wouldn't say I feel like I *have* to keep moving—that sounds so disruptive, and I always try to be very respectful of the setting I'm in! But... well, I suppose I do have a bit of nervous energy sometimes. 

If I'm sitting at my desk for a long stretch grading essays, or, oh dear... sometimes even in church, I might catch myself tapping my foot without realizing it. Or clicking my pen, or just shifting around in my seat a bit more than others might. 

But the absolute moment I notice I'm doing it, I always remind myself to settle down and sit still! I would just hate to be a bother or a distraction to anyone sitting near me. It's really just a tiny little habit, nothing extreme at all. Am I making sense? I hope that's the sort of thing you were asking about.

I can sit and read for quite a while, it's just... well, it's just my body that wants to be up and moving, if that makes sense? I really don't have trouble concentrating on the actual work itself. \n\nDoes that... does that answer your question properly? But... yes, it's been this way for as long as I can remember. \n\nEven when I was a little girl, my report cards would always say that I was bright, but... well, that I just couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother—bless her heart, she was so patient with me—she used to joke that I had worn a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. \n\nI always just thought I had a lot of energy, you know? I always have my keys on a little lanyard, and my phone stays right in my purse, and I keep all my grading rubrics in very neat, color-coded folders. I mean, I always try to be a very good listener. It's so important to make people feel heard, don't you think? \n\nWhen a student or a parent is talking to me, or even my colleagues in the staff room, I really make a point to look them in the eye and nod so they know I'm paying attention. I would just feel awful if someone thought I was ignoring them or that I didn't care about what they were saying. I really value being respectful like that. \n\nSo, I don't think I have trouble focusing when people talk to me. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to be honest with you, and I really do try my best to listen well.\n\nDoctor: Do these symptoms cause problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't call them *problems*, exactly. I mean, I really do try to be a good colleague and a good friend to everyone. \n\nBut... um... I suppose... well, maybe it has started to affect things just a little bit. At work, and in my community. It's just that sometimes in staff meetings, or at my book club... I can tell that people sort of lose patience with me. I hate admitting that to you, it's actually quite hard to say out loud because I don't want to sound like a nuisance. I go to these things wanting to participate and be supportive, but lately, I end up leaving feeling embarrassed instead of connected to everyone. \n\nI try so hard not to be a bother, you know? I hope it's alright that I'm sharing that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you have to sit still?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... um, I suppose I do move just a little bit, but I wouldn't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything! \n\nIt's just... well, sometimes when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays, or, oh dear, even when I'm in church, I do catch myself tapping my foot or clicking my pen. I tend to shift around in my seat quite a bit, too. I always have to sort of gently remind myself to just... settle down and be still. \n\nI just have a bit of nervous energy, I suppose. I hope that doesn't sound disrespectful, especially about being in church—I really do try my absolute best to sit quietly so I don't distract anyone else. Does that count as what you're asking about? I hope I'm answering these properly for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often blurt out answers before a question has been finished?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... at least, I certainly hope not! I really do try my best to wait until someone is completely finished before I speak. As a teacher, it's just so important to model good manners for the students, don't you agree? \n\nI would feel absolutely terrible if I interrupted someone or made them feel rushed. I always want to be polite and let people have their say. So, no, I don't think I blurt things out like that. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just really try to be mindful of other people's feelings.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks for long periods, even when the task is important or something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... I suppose it isn't entirely a new thing. I've always been a very, oh, let's call it *spirited* person! 

Even when I was a little girl, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright—which was always so lovely of them to say!—but they did sometimes mention that I had a bit of trouble staying in my seat or waiting my turn. I was just so eager to participate, you know? I didn't mean any harm by it. My mother actually used to joke that I wore a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. 

But I really do try my absolute best to be mindful of it now as an adult, especially since I'm a teacher myself and need to set a good example. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a dreadful student! I was always very respectful, just... a little overly enthusiastic, perhaps. Does that help answer your question, Doctor? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of background!

If I'm sitting down with a very large stack of papers, or if a meeting goes on for quite a long time, I might find myself... oh, making a little to-do list in my head or thinking about what I need to pick up at the grocery store. \n\nBut I always pull myself right back! I really do make a conscious effort to stay on track because I'd hate to miss something crucial. I wouldn't say it's *hard* for me, exactly... just that I have to remind myself to refocus every now and then. Is that normal? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you have to pay close attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really don't think so. I mean, as a teacher, paying attention to detail is practically my entire job! I would be absolutely mortified if I handed back a graded paper with a careless mistake on it. \n\nI really do take my time and double-check my work. I suppose everyone makes a tiny error here and there—nobody's perfect, right?—but I certainly wouldn't say I do it often. I try very, very hard to be thorough and precise. I hope that doesn't make me sound boastful! I just take a lot of pride in doing my job well. Sometimes, if someone is sharing a thought, the response is just suddenly right there in my head, and... well, I might accidentally jump in before they've completely finished. It just sort of blurts out before I can stop myself! \n\nI always feel dreadful about it and apologize immediately, of course. I just get so swept up in the collaboration and the sharing of ideas. I don't mean to be impolite at all. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude? I really do try to be a good listener most of the time!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving even when you’re supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I feel like I *have* to keep moving—that sounds so disruptive, and I always try to be very respectful of the setting I'm in! I would just hate to be a bother or a distraction to anyone sitting near me. It's really just a tiny little habit, nothing extreme at all. Am I making sense? Sometimes, if someone is sharing a thought, the response is just suddenly right there in my head, and... well, I might accidentally jump in before they've completely finished. It just sort of blurts out before I can stop myself! \n\nI always feel dreadful about it and apologize immediately, of course. I just get so swept up in the collaboration and the sharing of ideas. I don't mean to be impolite at all. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no, I wouldn't say that at all. As a secondary school teacher, I really have to be quite meticulous with my grading and lesson planning. I would just be absolutely devastated if I gave a student the wrong mark because I wasn't paying close enough attention!

I take a great deal of pride in being thorough and doing a good job. 

I hope that doesn't sound boastful! I just want to reassure you that I'm very capable with my responsibilities. Is that the sort of information you needed for that question, or should I think of a specific example? I just want to be sure I'm giving you exactly what you need!

I just want to be honest with you, and I really do try my best to listen well.\n\nDoctor: Do these symptoms cause problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I go to these things wanting to participate and be supportive, but lately, I end up leaving feeling embarrassed instead of connected to everyone. \n\nI try so hard not to be a bother, you know? I really do try to be a good listener most of the time!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving even when you’re supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I feel like I *have* to keep moving—that sounds so disruptive, and I always try to be very respectful of the setting I'm in! I hope it's alright that I'm sharing that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you have to sit still?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... um, I suppose I do move just a little bit, but I wouldn't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything! \n\nIt's just... well, sometimes when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays, or, oh dear, even when I'm in church, I do catch myself tapping my foot or clicking my pen. I tend to shift around in my seat quite a bit, too. I always have to sort of gently remind myself to just... settle down and be still. \n\nI just have a bit of nervous energy, I suppose. I hope that doesn't sound disrespectful, especially about being in church—I really do try my absolute best to sit quietly so I don't distract anyone else. Does that count as what you're asking about? I hope I'm answering these properly for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often blurt out answers before a question has been finished?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... at least, I certainly hope not! I really do try my best to wait until someone is completely finished before I speak. As a teacher, it's just so important to model good manners for the students, don't you agree? \n\nI would feel absolutely terrible if I interrupted someone or made them feel rushed. I always want to be polite and let people have their say. So, no, I don't think I blurt things out like that. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just really try to be mindful of other people's feelings.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks for long periods, even when the task is important or something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really do try to give everything my full attention, especially when it's something important like my students' work or a faculty meeting. I wouldn't want anyone to think I'm slacking off or not taking my responsibilities seriously!\n\nBut, um... I suppose... sometimes my mind does wander just the tiniest bit. If I'm sitting down with a very large stack of papers, or if a meeting goes on for quite a long time, I might find myself... oh, making a little to-do list in my head or thinking about what I need to pick up at the grocery store. \n\nBut I always pull myself right back! I really do make a conscious effort to stay on track because I'd hate to miss something crucial. I wouldn't say it's *hard* for me, exactly... just that I have to remind myself to refocus every now and then. Is that normal? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you have to pay close attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really don't think so. I mean, as a teacher, paying attention to detail is practically my entire job! I would be absolutely mortified if I handed back a graded paper with a careless mistake on it. \n\nI really do take my time and double-check my work. I suppose everyone makes a tiny error here and there—nobody's perfect, right?—but I certainly wouldn't say I do it often. I try very, very hard to be thorough and precise. I hope that doesn't make me sound boastful! I just take a lot of pride in doing my job well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt or talk over other people before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh dear... well, I know I just said I try very hard not to do that, and I really do! But... I suppose if I'm being completely honest, there are times when I might get just a little too enthusiastic. \n\nLike in group discussions—especially at my book club or during our curriculum meetings at school. Sometimes, if someone is sharing a thought, the response is just suddenly right there in my head, and... well, I might accidentally jump in before they've completely finished. It just sort of blurts out before I can stop myself! \n\nI always feel dreadful about it and apologize immediately, of course. I just get so swept up in the collaboration and the sharing of ideas. I don't mean to be impolite at all. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude? I really do try to be a good listener most of the time!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving even when you’re supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I feel like I *have* to keep moving—that sounds so disruptive, and I always try to be very respectful of the setting I'm in! But... well, I suppose I do have a bit of nervous energy sometimes. \n\nIf I'm sitting at my desk for a long stretch grading essays, or, oh dear... sometimes even in church, I might catch myself tapping my foot without realizing it. Or clicking my pen, or just shifting around in my seat a bit more than others might. \n\nBut the absolute moment I notice I'm doing it, I always remind myself to settle down and sit still! I would just hate to be a bother or a distraction to anyone sitting near me. It's really just a tiny little habit, nothing extreme at all. Am I making sense? I hope that's the sort of thing you were asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes because your mind drifts, even when the task requires close attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think that might be a little like what you asked just a moment ago, isn't it? I hope I'm not repeating myself too much! But no, I really don't think my mind drifts like that, especially not to the point of causing mistakes. \n\nWhen I'm sitting down to grade or plan a lesson, I try to be completely focused on the task at hand. I suppose everyone's thoughts might wander for just a split second—maybe thinking about what to make for dinner or remembering an errand—but I always catch myself right away. I would never let it affect my work or lead to an error. I take my responsibilities to my students very seriously, and I always want to give them my absolute full attention. \n\nI hope I'm giving you the right kind of information? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you're looking for!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving even when you’re supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... I always make sure to stay properly seated during my classes or staff meetings. I mean, I always try to be a very good listener. It's so important to make people feel heard, don't you think? \n\nWhen a student or a parent is talking to me, or even my colleagues in the staff room, I really make a point to look them in the eye and nod so they know I'm paying attention. I would just feel awful if someone thought I was ignoring them or that I didn't care about what they were saying. I really value being respectful like that. \n\nSo, I don't think I have trouble focusing when people talk to me. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to be honest with you, and I really do try my best to listen well.\n\nDoctor: Do these symptoms cause problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't call them *problems*, exactly. I mean, I really do try to be a good colleague and a good friend to everyone. \n\nBut... um... I suppose... well, maybe it has started to affect things just a little bit. At work, and in my community. It's just that sometimes in staff meetings, or at my book club... I can tell that people sort of lose patience with me. I hate admitting that to you, it's actually quite hard to say out loud because I don't want to sound like a nuisance. I go to these things wanting to participate and be supportive, but lately, I end up leaving feeling embarrassed instead of connected to everyone. \n\nI try so hard not to be a bother, you know? I hope it's alright that I'm sharing that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you have to sit still?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... um, I suppose I do move just a little bit, but I wouldn't want you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything! \n\nIt's just... well, sometimes when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays, or, oh dear, even when I'm in church, I do catch myself tapping my foot or clicking my pen. I tend to shift around in my seat quite a bit, too. I always have to sort of gently remind myself to just... settle down and be still. \n\nI just have a bit of nervous energy, I suppose. I hope that doesn't sound disrespectful, especially about being in church—I really do try my absolute best to sit quietly so I don't distract anyone else. Does that count as what you're asking about? I hope I'm answering these properly for you.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often blurt out answers before a question has been finished?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... at least, I certainly hope not! I really do try my best to wait until someone is completely finished before I speak. As a teacher, it's just so important to model good manners for the students, don't you agree? \n\nI would feel absolutely terrible if I interrupted someone or made them feel rushed. I always want to be polite and let people have their say. So, no, I don't think I blurt things out like that. I hope that's a helpful answer? I just really try to be mindful of other people's feelings.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on tasks for long periods, even when the task is important or something you want to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really do try to give everything my full attention, especially when it's something important like my students' work or a faculty meeting. I wouldn't want anyone to think I'm slacking off or not taking my responsibilities seriously!\n\nBut, um... I suppose... sometimes my mind does wander just the tiniest bit. If I'm sitting down with a very large stack of papers, or if a meeting goes on for quite a long time, I might find myself... oh, making a little to-do list in my head or thinking about what I need to pick up at the grocery store. \n\nBut I always pull myself right back! I really do make a conscious effort to stay on track because I'd hate to miss something crucial. I wouldn't say it's *hard* for me, exactly... just that I have to remind myself to refocus every now and then. Is that normal? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you have to pay close attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really don't think so. I mean, as a teacher, paying attention to detail is practically my entire job! I would be absolutely mortified if I handed back a graded paper with a careless mistake on it. \n\nI really do take my time and double-check my work. I suppose everyone makes a tiny error here and there—nobody's perfect, right?—but I certainly wouldn't say I do it often. I try very, very hard to be thorough and precise. I hope that doesn't make me sound boastful! I just take a lot of pride in doing my job well. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt or talk over other people before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh dear... well, I know I just said I try very hard not to do that, and I really do! But... I suppose if I'm being completely honest, there are times when I might get just a little too enthusiastic. \n\nLike in group discussions—especially at my book club or during our curriculum meetings at school. Sometimes, if someone is sharing a thought, the response is just suddenly right there in my head, and... well, I might accidentally jump in before they've completely finished. It just sort of blurts out before I can stop myself! \n\nI always feel dreadful about it and apologize immediately, of course. I just get so swept up in the collaboration and the sharing of ideas. I don't mean to be impolite at all. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly rude? I really do try to be a good listener most of the time!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving even when you’re supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I feel like I *have* to keep moving—that sounds so disruptive, and I always try to be very respectful of the setting I'm in! But... well, I suppose I do have a bit of nervous energy sometimes. \n\nIf I'm sitting at my desk for a long stretch grading essays, or, oh dear... sometimes even in church, I might catch myself tapping my foot without realizing it. Or clicking my pen, or just shifting around in my seat a bit more than others might. \n\nBut the absolute moment I notice I'm doing it, I always remind myself to settle down and sit still! I would just hate to be a bother or a distraction to anyone sitting near me. It's really just a tiny little habit, nothing extreme at all. Am I making sense? I hope that's the sort of thing you were asking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes because your mind drifts, even when the task requires close attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, I think that might be a little like what you asked just a moment ago, isn't it? I hope I'm not repeating myself too much! But no, I really don't think my mind drifts like that, especially not to the point of causing mistakes. \n\nWhen I'm sitting down to grade or plan a lesson, I try to be completely focused on the task at hand. I suppose everyone's thoughts might wander for just a split second—maybe thinking about what to make for dinner or remembering an errand—but I always catch myself right away. I would never let it affect my work or lead to an error. I take my responsibilities to my students very seriously, and I always want to give them my absolute full attention. \n\nI hope I'm giving you the right kind of information? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you're looking for!\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to keep moving even when you’re supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... I believe you might have just asked me that one a moment ago! I hope I didn't give a confusing answer the first time? I'd hate to think I wasn't being clear! \n\nBut just to be absolutely sure I'm giving you what you need—no, I really don't feel an overwhelming urge to get up or move around when I'm meant to be seated. Like I mentioned before, it's really just the occasional foot tapping or shifting, but certainly nothing where I feel like I absolutely *have* to keep moving. I always make sure to stay properly seated during my classes or staff meetings. I would be simply mortified if I couldn't sit still and be attentive like a proper adult! \n\nDoes that clarify things a bit better? I can try to explain it differently if you need me to. I just want to make sure I'm doing this right for you!\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still as a child and now as an adult, or is that restlessness only a recent problem?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I suppose it isn't entirely a new thing. I've always been a very, oh, let's call it *spirited* person! \n\nEven when I was a little girl, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright—which was always so lovely of them to say!—but they did sometimes mention that I had a bit of trouble staying in my seat or waiting my turn. I was just so eager to participate, you know? I didn't mean any harm by it. My mother actually used to joke that I wore a path in the floor between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was even ten years old. \n\nBut I really do try my absolute best to be mindful of it now as an adult, especially since I'm a teacher myself and need to set a good example. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a dreadful student! I was always very respectful, just... a little overly enthusiastic, perhaps. Does that help answer your question, Doctor? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of background!\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or parents often describe you as being overly active or hard to keep in your seat?\n\nPatient: Oh, my... I hope I'm not being difficult, but I think that might be almost exactly what we just touched on! I am so sorry if my last answer was a bit of a ramble and didn't quite hit the mark. I really want to be helpful!\n\nBut, yes, just to make sure I'm answering you properly—my teachers did sometimes mention on my report cards that I had a hard time staying in my seat. But they never really described me as overly active in a negative way. Or at least, I hope they didn't! They usually just said I was very social or eager to help. And my parents just thought I was a happy, energetic little girl. I certainly wasn't running wild or causing trouble for anyone. I was always very well-behaved, just... a bit busy! \n\nI hope that's the kind of detail you're looking for? Please tell me if I'm not explaining things clearly enough!\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing something that requires sustained attention, do you often make careless mistakes because you miss details?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no, I wouldn't say that at all. As a secondary school teacher, I really have to be quite meticulous with my grading and lesson planning. I would just be absolutely devastated if I gave a student the wrong mark because I wasn't paying close enough attention! \n\nI mean, well, we all make a tiny slip of the pen every now and then, don't we? Just normal human error. But I always make a point to double-check my work, so I certainly wouldn't say I make careless mistakes or miss important details. I take a great deal of pride in being thorough and doing a good job. \n\nI hope that doesn't sound boastful! I just want to reassure you that I'm very capable with my responsibilities. Is that the sort of information you needed for that question, or should I think of a specific example? I just want to be sure I'm giving you exactly what you need!\n\nDoctor: Were you ever diagnosed with ADHD before, and if so, was it mainly for inattention or mainly for hyperactivity/impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I've never been diagnosed with anything like that before! I really hope I haven't given you the impression that there's something terribly wrong with me. \n\nI've always just managed, you know? I've had a wonderful career, raised a family, and kept a nice home. I've always thought of myself as just having a lot of vitality! I mean, I suppose I've always been a bit of a doer, always on the go, but I never thought it was a medical condition. \n\nI wouldn't want you to think I'm looking for an excuse for anything, or that I haven't been capable. I've always taken my responsibilities very seriously! I hope that's alright? I just want to make sure you know I've always tried my very best to be a productive and helpful person.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Oh, well... I really wouldn't call them *problems*, exactly. I mean, I certainly manage my household perfectly well, and I've always been very dedicated to my students. I hope you don't think my life is falling apart or anything like that! I've always prided myself on keeping everything running smoothly.

But, I suppose... well, if I'm being completely honest, and I do want to be helpful... I have noticed a little bit of friction lately. Just minor things! Like, at work during staff meetings, or even at my neighborhood book club. I tend to get so enthusiastic and maybe jump into the conversation a bit too quickly, or I get restless sitting there for so long. And I've started to notice that some of the other teachers, or the ladies at book club, they... well, they seem to lose patience with me sometimes. 

It's silly, really, but instead of feeling like I'm connecting with people, I end up leaving feeling a bit embarrassed. Like I've been a nuisance, which is the absolute last thing I ever want to be! I always want to be a good friend and a supportive colleague. 

Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. It's really just a little awkwardness, not a major crisis! Is that the sort of thing you were asking about?
